# CGT Atlas — backlog-completion phase (RESUME POINT)

**What this is.** Temp durable buffer for the backlog-completion phase (user: deep Mity; user direction, session 29: "complete update backlogs — our goal is completion of this whole project first, in a coherent all-encompassing multi-chapter book"). /home/user scratch does not survive restarts, and sessions are being erased/retried often; this topic does. **On retry, the user says "resume backlog completing from knowledge topic temp buffer" — READ THIS FILE FIRST and resume from the STATUS LOG; never redo a completed step.** This topic persists across the sessions of this phase and is deleted only when the whole task list below is closed. Keep the Knowledge base tidy: this topic + `cgt-atlas-project` only — no other topics, no orphaned files; clean /tmp scratch at each session close.

**Protocol (house rules, unchanged).** (1) Machine-verify every claim before acting. (2) After every canvas edit: sync the master in `/home/user/knowledge/cgt-atlas-project/` and verify equality — byte-exact work via TypeScript file tools with string equality as oracle (bash proved byte-nondeterministic for large UTF-8 content; literal non-ASCII typed in bash strings arrives double-encoded). (3) Lecture bodies carry no session references (history → chapter appendix). (4) A chapter edit ⇒ rebuild + byte-exact verify its web edition. (5) Sources end with exactly one trailing \n. (6) Atlas: IN PROGRESS row before authoring; update after every unit; full record at session close. (7) Update this buffer after EVERY completed step. (8) Learner testing is the user's responsibility — never scheduled by the model; the user directs when it happens. (9) The PDF export pipeline is platform-side and blocked — out of model scope.

## CH. 12 SELECTION LEDGER (chunk 48a deliverable — the 30-problem slate; citations web-verified 2026-10-11)

**Citation-verification method + routes (reuse at authoring).** Year/problem statements verified via the Wayback Machine mirrors of the AoPS Wiki year pages (`https://web.archive.org/web/<stamp>/https://artofproblemsolving.com/wiki/index.php/<YYYY>_IMO_Problems` — the regex extraction works; stamps 2020/2021/2023/2024/2025 vary by year, retry if empty) and the OFFICIAL shortlist PDFs (`https://www.imo-official.org/assets/documents/problems/<YYYY>/IMO<YYYY>SL.pdf` — open_url extracts PDF text, truncated at ~32,793 chars; fine when the C-section sits early, e.g. 2011). BLOCKED ROUTES (recorded): AoPS direct + r.jina.ai proxy = Cloudflare "verify" pages; `imo-official.org/problems.aspx` ignores the ?year= parameter (JS-driven). All 30 slate citations below were confirmed against a fetched page (year page, per-problem page, official SL PDF, or multi-source search snippets: AoPS hit + independent second source where noted).

**Chapter structure DECIDED (editorial freedom, per the house frame).** Ch. 12 = "The Olympiad Bridge — Thirty Competition Problems, Worked in Atlas Patterns": frontmatter + intro (the Bridge's charter: every solution uses ONLY Atlas machinery, Ch. 1–11; no new mathematics) + How-to-read + a pattern-map table (which of P1–P26 the thirty problems exercise, with the honest-gaps note) + THREE TERRACES of 10 problems each (Terrace I "the doorstep rungs" / II "the working middle" / III "the summit rungs"), each problem = paraphrased statement (Atlas's own words; official citation under it) + "The handle" (pattern diagnosis) + worked solution + "What to learn"; Trick Bag + formula box at the end; Revision History & Verification Notes appendix; status footer. No graded-exercise set separate from the 30 (the problems ARE the course; difficulty ladder = the terraces, each problem tagged ●○○/●●○/●●● by rung).

**THE SLATE** (rung order; patterns primary+secondary; confidence + authoring-time flags; "fetch" = exact statement/number to re-verify at authoring time via the routes above):

Terrace I — doorstep rungs (●○○/●●○):
1. IMO 1987 P1 — permutations by fixed-point count: Σ k·p_k(n) = n! — P2 — statement FULLY verified (Wayback 1987 page). Solution: double count (π, fixed point); machine-trivial. CONFIDENT.
2. IMO 2005 P6 — 6-problem competition, every pair solved by > 2/5 of contestants, nobody solved all 6 ⟹ ≥ 2 contestants solved exactly 5 — P2 — FULLY verified. Double-count with strict inequality bookkeeping; machine: integer-feasibility census. CONFIDENT.
3. IMO 1964 P4 — 17 correspondents, 3 topics, one topic per pair ⟹ three pairwise on one topic — P9 (machinery: Ch. 10's R(3,3,3) = 17 both directions; cite the chapter) — FULLY verified. CONFIDENT.
4. IMO 1985 P4 — 1985 integers, all prime factors ≤ 23 ⟹ 4 distinct with product a 4th power — P9 + P25 (exponent-parity vectors over F₂; Ch. 6 Oddtown machinery) — FULLY verified (also Math.SE second source; generalization note: 1985 replaceable by 1537). Pair-up-then-match argument; machine: full analogue census (fewer primes/smaller counts). CONFIDENT.
5. IMO 1998 P2 — a contestants, b odd judges, pass/fail ratings, pairwise agreement ≤ k ⟹ k/a ≥ (b−1)/(2b) — P2 + P10 — gist verified; FETCH exact conclusion formula. Double count (judge pair, agreement); machine: small censuses + tightness witness. CONFIDENT.
6. IMO 2001 P3 — 21 boys + 21 girls, each solved ≤ 6 of 6 problems, every boy-girl pair shared a solved problem ⟹ some problem solved by ≥ 3 boys AND ≥ 3 girls — P2/P9 — gist verified (AoPS snippet); FETCH exact conclusion. Count (b, g, shared problem) triples two ways + optimization bound; machine: exhaustive check of the bounding argument. CONFIDENT.
7. ISL 2011 C2 — 1000 students in a circle ⟹ some k ∈ [100, 300] with a contiguous block of 2k whose halves hold equally many girls — P9 — FULLY verified (official SL PDF 2011). Sliding-window/prefix pigeonhole; MEDIUM: derive the exact window argument carefully at 48b.
8. IMO 2021 P1 — numbers 1..n on cards in two piles ⟹ one pile holds two cards summing to a perfect square — P9 + P25 (square-sum graph; an odd cycle defeats 2-coloring) — gist verified; FETCH exact n (recall 100). Machine: search the odd cycle in the square-sum graph; verify every pair on it is a square sum. CONFIDENT.
9. IMO 1995 P6 — p an odd prime; # of p-element subsets of {1..2p} with sum ≡ 0 mod p — answer (C(2p,p) − 2)/p + 2 — P1/P2 (cyclic-orbit counting; the two fixed sets are the exceptions) — FULLY verified. Machine: brute-force p = 3, 5, 7, 11 must reproduce the formula. CONFIDENT.
10. IMO 1986 P3 — integers on a regular pentagon, total positive, local flip operation ⟹ the process terminates — P4 (monovariant; recall Σx²-type decrease) — gist verified; FETCH the exact operation. Machine: simulate the exact operation + discover the decreasing potential on random instances. MEDIUM-CONFIDENT.

Terrace II — the working middle (●●○):
11. IMO 1985 P2 — k, n coprime; numbers 1..k+n−1 colored so that i and |i−k| (i ≠ k), i and |i−n| (i ≠ n) share colors ⟹ all one color — P4/P9 + Ch. 2 connectivity (residue graph connected via Bezout) — gist verified; FETCH exact index set. CONFIDENT.
12. IMO 1986 P6 — finite set of lattice points 2-colored so that on every axis-parallel line the color counts differ by ≤ 1 (always possible) — P16/P17 (pairing + alternating-path color flips; the Ch. 5 bridge problem) — gist verified; FETCH exact tolerance wording. Machine: matching-based construction census on random point sets. CONFIDENT.
13. IMO 1992 P3 — 9 points, no 4 coplanar; edges colored red/blue or left bare; the least n forcing a same-colored triangle is 33 — P9 + P25 (Ramsey + the α ≤ 5 matching argument; Ch. 3+10 machinery) — gist verified; FETCH exact wording. SELF-DERIVED answer 33: bare-edge graph must hit every K6 ⟹ α ≤ 5 ⟹ ≥ 4 bare edges impossible at n = 33; 32 achievable iff K9 minus a 4-edge matching 2-colors triangle-free — MUST machine-verify (coloring search). CONFIDENT-PENDING-MACHINE.
14. IMO 1996 P1 — rectangular board, moves between squares at center-distance √5, pass through every square once — P7 + P11 (parity coloring vs explicit tour) — gist verified; FETCH exact task + board size (answer recall: possible iff n even? UNVERIFIED). RISK FLAG: statement+answer both pending; drop to spares if it doesn't resolve at 48b.
15. IMO 2005 P2 — integer sequence with infinitely many ± terms, distinct residues mod every n ⟹ every integer occurs exactly once — P4/P9 — FULLY verified gist; machine: census of valid small sequences (expect the forced zigzag 0, 1, −1, 2, −2… up to shift) then induction proof. MEDIUM-CONFIDENT.
16. ISL 2011 C1 — n weights 2⁰..2^{n−1} placed on a balance one per move, first on the left, right pan never heavier; count the ways — P1/P4 — FULLY verified (official SL PDF). Machine: DP/BFS n ≤ 10 → the answer formula, then an induction proof. CONFIDENT (formula unknown yet — machine first, per house rule).
17. IMO 2019 P5 — Bank of Bath: n coins A/B in a row; if exactly k coins show A, flip the kth coin from the left; prove the process always stops — P4 (potential/invariant) — gist verified; FETCH exact phrasing. Machine: full census 2¹⁵ configurations (all terminate — verify) + potential discovery. CONFIDENT.
18. IMO 2010 P5 — six boxes B1..B6, one coin each; type 1: Bj → remove 1, add 2 to B_{j+1} (j ≤ 5); type 2: remove 1 from Bj (j ≤ 4), swap B_{j+1}, B_{j+2}; empty B1..B5 with B6 = N coins? — P4 (halving-weight invariant: Σ c_j 2^{−j}; type 1 preserves, type 2 strictly decreases; initial 63/64 ⟹ N ≤ 63; answer NO for the huge target) — FULLY verified statement (Wayback 2010 P5 page; author Hans Zantema); FETCH the exact target N (recall 2010^2010^2010, rendered blank by the wiki math strip). CONFIDENT-PENDING-N.
19. IMO 2009 P6 — grasshopper: distinct positive jump lengths a_1..a_n, forbidden set M of n−1 positive integers excluding the total; some order never lands in M — P4/P8 (greedy + swap induction) — FULLY verified (Wayback 2009 page, author stated). Machine: verify the swap argument on exhaustive small instances. CONFIDENT.
20. IMO 2020 P3 — pebbles of weights 1..4n (fetch exact), n colors, 4 pebbles per color; split into two piles of equal weight with exactly 2 of each color per pile — P4/P11 — gist verified; FETCH exact weight range. Machine: constructive search n ≤ 8 + verify the pairing/induction proof. MEDIUM-CONFIDENT.

Terrace III — the summit rungs (●●●):
21. IMO 2007 P3 — cliques of competitors; largest clique even ⟹ split into two rooms each with largest clique half the size (FETCH exact claim) — P8/P15 (vertex-splitting induction) — gist verified. MEDIUM.
22. IMO 2017 P5 — N(N+1) distinct-height soccer players in a row; remove N(N−1) leaving 2N where the (2k−1)th and (2k)th tallest remaining stand adjacent — P8/P11 (greedy from the tallest down; machine: small-N constructive census) — verified most of the statement (Eric Shen PDF + Wayback). CONFIDENT-PENDING-EXACT.
23. IMO 2023 P5 — Japanese triangle: row i has i circles, exactly one red; ninja path top→bottom; prove some ninja path meets ≥ [k] red circles (FETCH the exact bound; recall ≈ n/2) — P10 (averaging over paths/rotations) — gist verified (Grozev + Wayback). Machine: minimax over red placements, n ≤ 10 → k(n) formula. CONFIDENT-PENDING-EXACT.
24. IMO 2018 P3 — anti-Pascal triangle (each non-bottom number = |difference of the two below|); can all numbers be distinct? Answer NO — P2/P4 — gist + AoPS solution flavor verified; FETCH the exact "2018 different numbers" phrasing. Machine: exhaustive distinct-impossibility search n ≤ 5. CONFIDENT.
25. IMO 2017 P3 — hunter and invisible rabbit (equal speeds, plane); prove the hunter has a winning strategy — P11/P18 (explicit spiral strategy; pigeonhole) — gist verified; FETCH exact round rules. Machine: discrete small-board hunter-rabbit game values (verify the strategy principle in silico). MEDIUM-CONFIDENT.
26. IMO 2011 P2 — windmill: rotating line over a point set, pivot hand-off; some start visits every point as pivot infinitely often — P8 (extremal/continuity sweep) — FULLY verified (statement + the official 180°-sweep color-flip argument; also = ISL 2011 C3, dual-citable). Machine: exact-arithmetic sweep simulation on small point sets. CONFIDENT.
27. IMO 2018 P4 — 400 sites (20×20), Amy/Ben alternate placing red/blue stones, each new stone at distance ≠ √5? (FETCH the exact distance rule + who wins) — P18 (positional game; pairing/strategy argument) — gist verified; FETCH exact. RISK FLAG pending the official rule; machine: small-board exhaustive game search.
28. IMO 2012 P3 — the liar's guessing game (n ≥ 2 cards, one secret, yes/no answers, at most one lie; determine all n where the player wins) — P4/P18 — citation verified (multiple sources; dgrozev); FETCH exact rules + RE-DERIVE the answer from scratch (my recall of the answer is UNRELIABLE — do not trust it; solve fresh, machine where possible). RISK FLAG: drop to spares if not clean.
29. IMO 2022 P1 — Bank of Oslo: n aluminium + n bronze coins in a row; chains; given k, find the least number of swaps (adjacent different-type pairs?) making every chain length ≤ k (FETCH exact task + answer) — P8 (greedy/monovariant) — gist verified (Wayback 2022 page). MEDIUM-PENDING-EXACT; machine: BFS small n → formula.

IMO 1978 P6 — 1978-member international society from 6 countries; some member's number = sum of two (or double of one) from his own country — P2/P9 (Ch. 10 Schur bridge: 6 sum-free sets cannot cover [1, 1978]) — statement FULLY verified. SOLUTION RE-DERIVATION REQUIRED (elementary 1978 solution, NOT via S(6) = 536 which was only proven in 2017): difference-set counting — derive at 48b. RISK FLAG: replacements ready if stuck.

**CHECKPOINT 51b-1 ✅ — web canon-pin verification complete (2026-10-12; two search batches). Pins (statements + citations verified):** FKG = Fortuin–Kasteleyn–Ginibre 1971, Comm. Math. Phys. 22, 89–103 (finite distributive lattice; μ log-supermodular = the lattice condition μ(x∧y)μ(x∨y) ≥ μ(x)μ(y); increasing f,g ⟹ positively correlated; same inequality reversed for one-up-one-down; Holley 1974 as the proof route; Ahlswede–Daykin 1978 four-functions ⟹ FKG); Kleitman 1966 “Families of non-disjoint subsets”, JCT 1, 153–155 (the Harris–Kleitman up-set correlation, product measures); Janson 1990 “Poisson approximation for large deviations”, RSA 1(2) 221–229 (Janson’s inequality; appeared in Janson–Łuczak–Ruciński 1990 *Random Graphs*; tail form P(X ≤ λ−t) ≤ exp(−φ(−t/λ)λ²/Δ̄) ≤ exp(−t²/(2Δ̄)); “Janson I tells nothing when Δ ≫ μ”; Janson–Knuth–Łuczak–Pittel 1993 “The birth of the giant component”, RSA 4 231–358); Spencer 1985 “Six standard deviations suffice”, Trans. AMS 289(2) 679–706 (n sets on n elements ⟹ a 2-coloring with discrepancy ≤ K√n, K absolute = 6; partial coloring); Bregman–Minc (Minc conjecture 1963, Brégman 1973; per A ≤ ∏(rᵢ!)^{1/rᵢ}; equality = block-diagonal all-ones up to row/col permutations; entropy proofs by Schrijver + Radhakrishnan); Moser–Tardos 2010 algorithmic LLL (Gödel Prize 2020; the resampling algorithm + the compression/entropy-compression argument; symmetric condition e·p·(d+1) ≤ 1 per the Ch. 4 §4.3 convention); Talagrand 1995 “Concentration of measure and isoperimetric inequalities in product spaces”, Publ. Math. IHÉS 81, 73–205 (convex-distance inequality, e^{−t²/4} flavor); Shamir–Spencer 1987, Combinatorica 7, 121–129 (sharp concentration of χ(G(n,p)); fixed p: √n·ω(n) consecutive integers; Azuma vertex-exposure form P(|χ−Eχ| > λ√(n−1)) < 2e^{−λ²/2}; average degree < n^{1/6}: five consecutive integers); Alon–Krivelevich 1997 Combinatorica 17, 303–313 (χ concentration); Banaszczyk 1998 RSA (vectors ℓ₂ ≤ 1/5 into a convex body of Gaussian measure ½); Lovett–Meka 2015 (constructive partial colorings, Beck’s entropy method recovered); Bansal 2010 SDP-guided walk; Erdős–Rényi 1959 “On random graphs I” Publ. Math. Debrecen + 1960 “On the evolution of random graphs” Publ. Math. Inst. Hung. Acad. Sci. 5 (G(n,M) evolution, connectivity); Gilbert 1959 (G(n,p), connectivity threshold); Bollobás 1984 (the critical window n^{−4/3}); van der Waerden permanent conjecture (Egorychev/Falikman 1981: doubly stochastic ⟹ per ≥ n!/nⁿ); Kahn 2001 (list-chromatic index asymptotics); the Komlós conjecture + the unsatisfiable-matrices lower-bound line (disc of column-normalized matrices).

**CHECKPOINT 52b-1 ✅ — web canon-pin verification complete (2026-10-12; four search batches). Pins (statements + citations verified):**
- **Szemerédi regularity lemma** — E. Szemerédi, “Regular partitions of graphs”, *Problèmes Combinatoires et Théorie des Graphes* (Orsay 1976), Colloq. Internat. CNRS 260, 399–401. Statement (modern form): for every ε > 0 and m there is M(ε, m) such that every graph admits an equitable partition into k parts, m ≤ k ≤ M, with all but ε·k² pairs ε-regular. **Gowers lower bound: M(ε) ≥ a tower of twos of height polynomial in 1/ε** (height Ω(ε^{-1/16})); Moshkovitz–Shapira’s tight lower bound (“A tight lower bound for Szemerédi’s regularity lemma”, Combinatorica) + their short proof of Gowers’ bound (arXiv:1308.5352); the standard algorithmic proof = iterated refinement with the energy (index) increasing by ≥ ε⁵ per defect-refinement step.
- **Counting lemma** — standard: for an ε-regular partition, the fraction of triples of (Vi, Vj, Vk) forming triangles ≈ the product of densities d_ij·d_ik·d_jk; “either zero or Ω(n³) copies — no in-between”; proof by greedy embedding of H’s vertices one by one (the chapter proves it in full).
- **Graph removal lemma** — statement (Fox 2011 abstract, verified verbatim): every graph on n vertices with o(n^h) copies of a fixed h-vertex H can be made H-free by removing o(n²) edges. Quantitative honestly: regularity-based proofs force δ = 1/tower(ε^{-O(1)}) — the true δ(ε) is barely known (the Conlon–Fox survey note). **Fox, “A new proof of the graph removal lemma”, Annals of Mathematics 174(1) (2011), DOI 10.4007/annals.2011.174.1.17 (arXiv:1006.1300)** — avoids the regularity lemma; better bounds for directed/multicolored analogues.
- **Triangle removal + Ruzsa–Szemerédi** — the Ruzsa–Szemerédi problem (the (6,3)-problem): the maximum number of edges of a graph in which every edge belongs to exactly one triangle (equivalently: balanced bipartite graphs whose edges partition into a linear number of induced matchings); the 1978 initial formulation was slightly weaker (“locally linear”); RS established: for every η > 0 there is c > 0 such that every sufficiently large graph with at most c·n³ triangles can be made triangle-free by deleting ≤ η·C(n,2) edges. Canonical citation: Ruzsa–Szemerédi, “Triple systems with no six points spanning three triangles”, Combinatorica 1 (1981) — title/journal/year by strong recall + content confirmed by the survey record; **re-verify pages at authoring before quoting** (honest flag).
- **Erdős–Stone** — Erdős–Stone, “On the structure of linear graphs”, Bull. Amer. Math. Soc. 52(12) (1946), 1087–1091 (Project Euclid-confirmed). Turán-density form: π(H) = 1 − 1/(χ(H)−1); ex(n, H) determined up to o(n²). **Compact form: Erdős–Simonovits, “A limit theorem in graph theory”, Studia Sci. Math. Hungar. 1 (1966), 51–57.**
- **Stability** — Simonovits, “A Method for Solving Extremal Problems in Graph Theory”, in *Theory of Graphs* (Proc. Colloq. Tihany 1966), 1968, pp. 279–319: near-extremal H-free graphs become (χ(H)−1)-partite after deleting o(n²) edges; derivable from the regularity lemma (the modern route the chapter will sketch after proving Erdős–Stone from it).
- **Roth** — Roth, “On certain sets of integers”, J. London Math. Soc. s1-28(1) (1953), 104–109, DOI 10.1112/jlms/s1-28.1.104. Triangle removal ⟹ Roth: the standard reduction — the chapter gives it in full and machine-checks the layered-graph correspondence at small scale (52c). **Behrend 1946** = the lower-bound construction (barely improved since; the Salem–Spencer precursor; the Kelley–Meka 2023 improvement noted honestly, statement-level only).
- **Szemerédi’s theorem** — Szemerédi, “On sets of integers containing k elements in arithmetic progression”, Acta Arith. 27(1) (1975), 199–245 (EUDML-confirmed). History: k = 3 Roth 1953; k = 4 Szemerédi (combinatorial) with a second proof by Roth 1972; the general case 1975 (“a masterpiece of combinatorial reasoning”).
- **Hypergraph removal** — Nagle–Rödl–Schacht + Gowers, independently (Nagle, “The counting lemma for regular k-uniform hypergraphs”, Random Struct. Algorithms 28 (2006); Gowers’ quasirandomness program); implies Szemerédi’s theorem and the multidimensional version (Furstenberg–Katznelson) — **statement-level only in the chapter** (the honest boundary of the removal route).
- **Density Hales–Jewett** — statement: for δ > 0, k, and n > n₀(δ, k), every A ⊆ [k]^n with |A| ≥ δk^n contains a combinatorial line; **implies Szemerédi’s theorem** (verified). D. H. J. Polymath, “A new proof of the density Hales–Jewett theorem”, Annals of Mathematics 175(3) (2012), 1283–1327 (arXiv:0910.3926). The coloring-form HJ generalizes van der Waerden — the Ch. 10 cross-ref (the HJ statement already shipped there). The hierarchy vdW < Sz < density HJ (each implies the next) recorded; the chapter shows the chain at statement level.
- **Cap set** — Croot–Lev–Pach, “Progression-free sets in Z_4^n are exponentially small”, Annals 185 (2017); Ellenberg–Gijswijt, “On large subsets of F_q^n with no three-term arithmetic progression”, Annals 185 (2017): **|cap| ≤ 2.756^n in F_3^n** (the headline constant confirmed; the exact EG constant ≈ 2.7551046… to be computed machine-side at 52c). Prior bounds honestly: Meshulam 2001 (Fourier/density increment, O(3^n/n)); Bateman–Katz 2012 (3^n/n^{1+ε}). Slice-rank symmetric formulation (Tao’s blog record). **The Ch. 6 cross-ref anchor: CLP/EG is the polynomial method deployed on the symmetric power — the direct descendant of Ch. 6 L3 (RCW/FW, diagonal restrictions); the exact pointer to be re-aimed at authoring.**
- **Design locks feeding 52c (machine runs — plan):** (1) regularity machinery: an ε-regular-pair checker + the energy (index) increment verified on small graphs (defect refinement ⇒ Δenergy ≥ ε⁵, small-scale exhaustive); (2) counting-lemma empirical: planted-density graphs at small n — triangle counts vs density products; (3) triangle removal at brute-force scale: max triangles vs minimum triangle-cover trade-off on small graphs + the Ruzsa–Szemerédi induced-matching partition census on small bipartite graphs; (4) the Roth reduction machine-check: the layered graph built from a 3-AP-free set — verify the triangle/AP correspondence at small scale; (5) cap sets: exhaustive maximum caps in F_3^n for n = 1..5 (known maxima 2/4/9/20/45 by recall — machine-verify, never trust recall) + the EG constant computed exactly + the trinomial-coefficient bound checked against the exhaustive maxima; (6) Erdős–Stone/stability witnesses: Turán graphs + perturbations at small n vs (1 − 1/(r−1))C(n,2); (7) the Gowers tower arithmetic honesty exhibit (compute the standard bound formula at sample ε).

## CH. 17 CHUNK 54b WORKING LOG (canon pins — AUTHORITATIVE for authoring chunk 54e; on retry resume at the last ✅ line — never redo a completed sub-step)

**CHECKPOINT 54b-1 ✅ — web canon-pin verification complete (batches 1–4, web, 2026-10-12) + the Ch. 2/6/8 cross-ref anchors read from the masters. Honest catches flagged [ ].**

**L1 — Pólya/Burnside (pays Ch. 2's X1 seed):**
- Pólya enumeration theorem: first published Redfield (1927), independently rediscovered + popularized by Pólya (1937), "Kombinatorische Anzahlbestimmungen für Gruppen, Graphen und chemische Verbindungen"; aka Redfield–Pólya; cycle-index form |Y^X/G| = (1/|G|)·Σ_g m^{c(g)}.
- Burnside's lemma: Cauchy (1845) / Frobenius (1887); Burnside's 1897 book quoted it attributing Frobenius; "the lemma that is not Burnside's" (Stigler's law); aka Cauchy–Frobenius / orbit-counting lemma.
- Ch. 2 X1 seed (read from the master): tree shape-counts 1, 1, 1, 2, 3, 6 (n = 1..6) vs Cayley n^{n−2}; the exploration explicitly names "Pólya's machine" as the future payer. [54c: machine-verify shape counts n ≤ 8 (1,1,1,2,3,6,11,23 — A000055) + a brute-force Burnside cross-check.]

**L2 — Möbius inversion:**
- Rota (1964), "On the foundations of combinatorial theory I. Theory of Möbius functions", Z. Wahrscheinlichkeitstheorie verw. Gebiete 2, 340–368; poset μ + the incidence algebra; classical number-theoretic μ = the divisor-lattice case; τ/φ identities as the worked examples. [54c: machine-verify Σ_{d|n} 1 = τ(n) and Σ_{d|n} d·μ(n/d) = φ(n) for n ≤ 10⁴.]

**L3 — designs I (Steiner, Fisher, projective planes):**
- Kirkman: first publication 1846 (Cambridge and Dublin Math. Journal, solving Woolhouse's 1844 Lady's and Gentleman's Diary problem); Steiner's 1853 paper named the systems. [DATE NUANCE: MathWorld dates the existence theorem "Kirkman 1847" — the paper appeared in the journal's 1847 volume; cite "Kirkman (1846/47)" in the chapter.]
- STS(v) exists iff v ≡ 1 or 3 (mod 6) — the only restriction; sufficiency Bose (1939)/Skolem (1958).
- Kirkman's schoolgirl problem: posed 1850 (15 girls, 7 days); Sylvester's 13-week extension solved by Denniston (1974, Discrete Math. 9, 229–233; 7 hours on an Elliott 4130; uniqueness still open).
- **HONEST SCOPE CATCH (load-bearing): S(2,3,6) does NOT exist** — λ(v−1)/(k−1) = 5/2 non-integer; the chapter treats it as the worked non-existence exhibit (machine proof at 54c).
- Projective plane of order q = S(2, q+1, q²+q+1); affine plane of order q = S(2, q, q²).
- Fisher (1940), "An examination of the different possible solutions of a problem in incomplete blocks", Annals of Eugenics 10, 52–87 [pages per the standard citation]: a non-trivial (v,k,λ)-design has λ(v−1) ≥ k(k−1) (equivalently b ≥ v); Bose's elegant alternative proof noted. Ch. 6 cross-ref (read from the master): Lecture 1 "Fisher's Inequality, Two Ways" — the Fano Gram det 576 = 24², Bose m ≤ n+1, the near-pencil — Fisher is tight at m = n.
- Bruck–Ryser (1949) projective planes + Chowla–Ryser (1950) symmetric designs: order q ≡ 1,2 (mod 4) not a sum of two squares ⇒ no plane; rules out orders 6 and 14; order 10 non-existence = coding theory + large computer search (Lam, Swiercz & Thiel, 1989); order 12 = the smallest open.
- STS non-isomorphic counts: STS(7) = 1 (Fano), STS(9) = 1, STS(13) = 2, STS(15) = 80, STS(19) = 11,084,874,829 (Kaski–Östergård 2004 classification; Stinson–Ferch 1985 forerun). [54c: brute-force STS(7) uniqueness + STS(13) = 2; STS(15) NOT to be enumerated.]

**L4 — designs II (Wilson, Keevash, SQS):**
- Wilson: early 1970s existence theory for pairwise balanced designs — designs exist for all but finitely many v given k ("An existence theory for pairwise balanced designs" I–III + the survey "Constructions and Uses of Pairwise Balanced Designs").
- Keevash, "The existence of designs", arXiv 1401.3665 (2014, v4 2024): proves the existence conjecture, answering Steiner's 1853 question — S(t,k,n) exists for all large n whenever the divisibility conditions hold; non-constructive; also settles nontrivial Steiner systems for t ≥ 6 + infinitely many t = 4,5.
- SQS(v) = S(3,4,v) exists iff v ≡ 2 or 4 (mod 6): Hanani (1960, Canad. J. Math. 12, 145–157) + second proof Hanani (1963, Canad. J. Math. 15, 702–722; H-designs introduced there); Barrau (1908): uniqueness of S(3,4,8) and S(3,4,10); SQS(16) count 1,054,163 (batch-2 pin). S(4,5,n): unique at n = 11; none at 15, 17; known at 23, 35, 47, 71, ...; smallest open order 21.

**L5 — coding beyond Hamming (the Ch. 8 cross-ref):**
- Singleton: R. C. Singleton (1964), "Maximum distance q-nary codes", IEEE Trans. Inf. Theory 10(2), 116–118 — A_q(n,d) ≤ q^{n−d+1}. [PRIORITY CATCH: proven earlier by Joshi (1958), and by Komamiya (1953) — record honestly in the chapter, cite Singleton as the standard.] MDS = equality; binary MDS codes only the trivial ones (repetition, parity, whole space); nontrivial MDS = Reed–Solomon + extensions.
- MDS conjecture: proposed as a question by Segre (1955); statement — a nontrivial [n,k]_q MDS code has n ≤ q+1, except q even with k = 3 or k = q−1 where n ≤ q+2; prime case PROVEN: Ball (2012), J. Eur. Math. Soc. 14, 733–748; Ball–De Beule (2012), Des. Codes Cryptogr. 65, 5–14 (k ≤ 2p−2 for q = p^h); general case OPEN. [Chapter states it as a conjecture with the prime-case theorem.]
- Plotkin (1960), "Binary codes with specified minimum distance", IRE Trans. Inf. Theory 6(4), 445–450: A_2(n,d) ≤ 2⌊d/(2d−n)⌋ when 2d > n; A_2(2d,d) ≤ 4d. Ch. 8 already owns the Delsarte-certificate form (F = 1 + K₁/(2d−n); all 29 pairs with 2d > n, 2 ≤ n ≤ 10 machine-checked; exact LPs A(7,3)=16, A(7,4)=8, A(8,4)=16, A(8,5)=4; the honest (8,3): LP 128/5 vs truth 20) — cite forward, do NOT re-prove.
- Gilbert (1952), "A comparison of signalling alphabets", Bell System Tech. J. 31(3), 504–522 (greedy, general codes); Varshamov (1957), "Estimate of the number of signals in error correcting codes", Dokl. Akad. Nauk SSSR 117, 739–741 (linear codes); GV: A_q(n,d) ≥ q^n / Σ_{j=0}^{d−1} C(n,j)(q−1)^j; q-ary entropy asymptotics H_q. [54c: GV vs truth on small parameters; a greedy-construction witness.]

**L6 — codes ↔ designs:**
- Golay (1949), "Notes on Digital Coding", Proc. IRE 37, 657 — Berlekamp's "best single published page" in coding theory; the ternary code found independently by Virtakallio (1947, the football magazine Veikkaaja). [COLOR ANECDOTE: verified.]
- Extended binary Golay [24,12,8]; perfect binary Golay [23,12,7]; 4096 codewords; weights 0, 8, 12, 16, 24; 759 = 3·11·23 octads — the blocks of S(5,8,24); 2576 dodecads; unique up to relabeling; Aut = M24, order 2^10·3^3·5·7·11·23 = 244,823,040; M23, M22, M12, M11 as stabilizers; M24 and M12 5-transitive (M12 sharply; M11 sharply 4-transitive; M23 4-transitive; M22 3-transitive — by CFSG the only 4-transitive permutation groups beyond S/A are the Mathieu groups).
- S(5,6,12) unique, Aut = M12; weight-6 supports of the extended ternary Golay [12,6,6] form S(5,6,12).
- Ternary Golay [11,6,5]: perfect, 729 codewords, the QR code of length 11 over F₃; extended [12,6,6] self-dual (Gleason weight enumerator). [PHRASING CATCH: the weight-5 words of the ternary code support the Witt S(4,5,11) — 66 blocks, each the support of exactly 2 codewords (132 weight-5 words); Wikipedia phrases the weight-5 set as a 3-(11,5,4) design (signed multiplicity). Pin the 66/132 counts + the 4-design property at machine time.]
- Assmus–Mattson (1969), "New 5-designs", J. Combin. Theory 6, 122–151: the method for finding t-designs in linear codes (the classic 5-designs on 12 and 24 points = the Witt S(5,6,12)/S(5,8,24) via the Golay codes).
- Ch. 8 Delsarte cross-ref (read from the master): Lecture 4 §4.4 "Delsarte's program: the polynomial method on the Hamming cube" — the [7,4] Hamming distribution [1,0,0,7,7,0,0,1], Delsarte inequalities Σ A_i·K_k(i) ≥ 0. [54c: the Assmus–Mattson code-derived-designs bridge.]

**CHECKPOINT 48b-6 ✅ — PROBLEM 7 (ISL 2011 C2) VERIFIED COMPLETE — the window proof DERIVED from scratch and machine-verified at every link. (The official solution was NOT fetchable: AoPS-direct/Math.SE/yumpu/slideshare = Cloudflare- or JS-blocked; the official SL PDF truncates before the C-solutions; no Wayback snapshot of the AoPS wiki C2 page. The derivation below is authoritative for authoring.)**

- **THE PROOF (P7 = ISL 2011 C2).** D_k(a) := girls in [a, a+k) − girls in [a+k, a+2k), indices mod 1000; goal ∃ k ∈ [100,300], a with D_k(a) = 0. Three load-bearing identities: (I1) D_K(a+1) − D_K(a) = 2g_{a+K} − g_a − g_{a+2K}; (I2) D_{K+1}(a) − D_K(a) = 2g_{a+K} − g_{a+2K} − g_{a+2K+1}; (I3) D_{K−1}(a) − D_K(a) = −2g_{a+K−1} + g_{a+2K−2} + g_{a+2K−1}. Jump rule: 2v − u − w = −2 ⟺ (u,v,w) = (1,0,1) (exhaustive 2³). Assume D_k(a) ≠ 0 for ALL k ∈ [100,300], a. (1) Family k = 100: Σ_a D_100(a) = 0 (each position lies in exactly 100 first halves and 100 second halves over the 1000 starts) with no zeros ⟹ both signs present; by (I1) adjacent steps ≤ 2, so the cyclic walk changes sign only through adjacent (1,−1)/(−1,1) pairs, and BOTH orientations occur (a +→− crossing must step exactly +1→−1; cyclically crossings alternate) ⟹ ∃ a: D_100(a) = 1, D_100(a+1) = −1. (2) Jump rule on (I1): (g_a, g_{a+100}, g_{a+200}) = (1, 0, 1). (3) MIGRATION INDUCTION: for j = 0..198, family 100+j carries the jump at the pair (a−j, a−j+1) — D_{100+j}(a−j) = 1, D_{100+j}(a−j+1) = −1 — with triple (g_{a−j}, g_{a+100}, g_{a+200+j}) = (1,0,1). Step j→j+1 (K = 100+j, b = a−j): by (I2), D_{K+1}(b) = 1 + 2g_{a+100} − g_{a+200+j} − g_{a+200+j+1} = −g_{a+200+j+1}; nonzero ⟹ g_{a+200+j+1} = 1 and D_{K+1}(b) = −1. Then by (I1) at family K+1: D_{K+1}(b) − D_{K+1}(b−1) = 2g_{a+100} − g_{a−j−1} − g_{a+200+j+1} = −g_{a−j−1} − 1 ⟹ D_{K+1}(b−1) = g_{a−j−1}; nonzero ⟹ g_{a−j−1} = 1, D_{K+1}(b−1) = 1. So the jump re-appears at family K+1 at the pair (b−1, b) = (a−j−1, a−j) — the position marches DOWN one step per family; the triple's middle boy g_{a+100} = 0 is what makes both 2g-terms vanish. (4) After j = 0..198: g ≡ 1 on the whole run a−199..a — 200 CONSECUTIVE GIRLS (the induction also forces the far run a+200..a+399). (5) The contiguous group of 200 students a−199..a is all girls: first half 100 girls, second half 100 girls ⟹ D_100(a−199) = 0 — a balanced block with k = 100 ∈ [100,300]. CONTRADICTION. ∎ RANGE USE: the migration consumes families 100..299 (all inside [100,300]); the kill uses k = 100. Chapter remark: the range WIDTH is the engine — with range [100,250] the migration would only force 151 consecutive girls and the kill fails.
- **Machine receipts (all PASS):** (I1)/(I2)/(I3) verified on 10 random circles × 300 (K,a) samples each; jump rule exhaustive; sign-change lemma exhaustive over all cyclic integer walks (length ≤ 5, values ±{1,2,3}, steps ≤ 2, both signs): both orientations always present; the 199-step propagation run symbolically on tri-state g (n = 1000, a = 500): ZERO conflicts, forces girls at 301..500 (the 200-run), boy at 600, girls at 700..899, and D_100(301) = 0 confirmed by direct count; the concrete circle realizing the propagated structure verified (D_100(301) = 0); empirically, blocks125's genuinely zero-free family 100 carries both jump orientations and EVERY jump satisfies the (1,0,1)/(0,1,0) triple rule; the theorem itself verified on 60+ adversarial/random circles — every one has zeros for ≥ 101 of the 201 k's (hardest: the alternating circle, only even k); zero-free k-families of block-alternating circles follow the modular pattern k mod 2B ∈ [⌈B/2⌉, ⌊3B/2⌋] with parity corrections (e.g. blocks250/B even admits k = B). AUTHORING NOTE: tag P9 + the discrete-continuity flavor; the migrating-jump-through-the-parameter-range is the Bridge's teaching gem — a What-to-learn in its own right. The buffer's original hint ("average 0 per k; steps ∈ [−2,2]; the range must be used jointly") is vindicated — the migration induction IS the joint use.

(Chapter proof route: Bezout/Euclid walk — the two involutions i ↦ n−i and i ↦ |i−k| generate the full symmetric walk when gcd = 1; machine census is the authority.)
- **P10 = 1986 P3 VERIFIED — monovariant DERIVED:** Φ = Σ x_i² + Σ (x_i + x_{i+1})² (= 3Σx² + 2Σx_ix_{i+1}). ΔΦ = 2·y_middle·S exactly (y = middle of the operated triple, S = total sum > 0) — verified on 20,000 random 5-vectors × all 5 positions (sum preserved); 3,000 random simulations ALL terminate (max 128 steps), Φ strictly decreasing by 2|y|S ≥ 2 each step. Derivation for authoring: for a dihedral-symmetric quadratic form Q = aΣx² + bΣx_ix_{i+1} + cΣx_ix_{i+2}, the operation's displacement d = (1,−2,1,0,0) gives ΔQ = y[2B(x,d) + yQ(d)] where 2B(x,d) has EQUAL coefficients on the operated triple (x,y,z) and equal on the two outer vertices (u,v); forcing all five equal (2a−3b+2c = 0) yields ΔQ = y·βS — take Q = 3Σx² + 2Σx_ix_{i+1} (β = 2). Termination: Φ integer ≥ 0, decreases by ≥ 2S ≥ 2 → at most Φ₀/(2S) operations.
- **P12 = 1986 P6 VERIFIED — constructive algorithm:** rows ∪ columns as vertices of a bipartite multigraph, points as edges. If some vertex has degree 1, peel that point (induct on the rest; color the point to fix its OTHER line — a ±1 imbalance becomes 0, a 0 becomes ±1). If all degrees ≥ 2, extract a SIMPLE cycle (alternating row/col vertices), delete its points, induct, color the cycle's points alternately (each involved row/column has exactly two cycle points, opposite colors). Census: 5,008 instances (5,000 random, ≤ 30 points on an 8×8 grid + 8 adversarial: single point, one row, diagonal, 7×11 lattice, full 6×6, checkerboard, two rows, three columns) — ALL balanced within 1 on every row and column. [Lesson recorded: my first two implementations were buggy (an Euler-trail walk that could revisit vertices; then a DFS that overwrote arrival-edge records) — the census caught both; the final grey-node simple-cycle DFS is clean. Machine-first discipline paid off.]
- **Operational discovery (applies to ALL future 48x runs):** run_typescript now enforces maxProgramOperations = 1024 — Math.random()/Date.now() calls count toward the budget (plain loop iterations do not). Use deterministic LCGs for pseudo-randomness (also replay-friendly). A 20000-trial monovariant check + 3000 simulations + 5000-instance census all fit easily otherwise.

- **P16 ISL 2011 C1 official cross-check ✓:** Evan Chen's 2011 notes state "The answer is a_n = (2n−1)!!" — CONFIRMS the machine DP answer.
- **P20 pebbles (Evan Chen 2020 notes) VERIFIED:** key fact 1+4n = 2+(4n−1) = 3+(4n−2) = ⋯; pair the weights (k, 4n+1−k), each pair summing to 4n+1; boxes = colors, strings = pair-edges ⟹ 4-regular multigraph on n vertices (self-loops allowed, count degree 2); each component has an Euler circuit (k vertices ⟹ 2k edges); color the circuit's edges alternately ⟹ every vertex gets 2 blue + 2 green ends; pile 1 = endpoints of blue edges, pile 2 = green ⟹ each pile automatically weighs n(4n+1) with 2 pebbles of each color. Machine: 1200/1200 random colorings (n=2..7, 200 each) ✓ + witness n=2 coloring A,A,B,B,A,B,A,B ✓.
- **P19 grasshopper official induction (Evan Chen 2009 notes — complete 4-case proof) VERIFIED as a constructive algorithm:** WLOG jumps ascending; mines = M; x = s − a_max. Case 1 (x clear, a mine past x): ≤ n−2 mines below x ⟹ IH to x, then a_max lands at s. Cases 2/3 (no mine right of x): IH on M \ {m} (m = max mine) avoids everything except possibly m; if the path lands on m via hop a_k, SWAP that hop with a_max — the new landings m−a_k+a_max > m and later shifted landings stay > m ≥ every mine, and a_k goes LAST landing exactly at s (needs a_max > a_k ✓ by distinctness). Case 4 (x mined AND a mine past x): the mine x itself is NOT among the 2(n−1) points {s−a_i, s−a_i−a_max : i<n} ⟹ some i has BOTH clear (pigeonhole); ≥ 2 mines above y = s−a_i−a_max (namely x and the mine past x) ⟹ IH with ≤ n−3 mines to y, then y → s−a_i → s. Machine: consecutive-jump census n=2..6, ALL M: 12,406/12,406 valid; random distinct jump sets n=2..9, 400 each: 3,200/3,200 valid. GOTCHA: rest-space vs jumps-space index remapping (the first version passed the census only because sorted jumps made the two spaces coincide — 337 random fails exposed it).
